Notice that we already proved that the orthonormalization (10) and normalization (11) operations are valid retractions for the orthogonal and oblique manifolds, respectively. To show that R POB X is another valid retraction for the orthoblique manifold, we just need to show that the projection of rotation blocks onto the orthogonal space of previous rotation blocks also satisfies the retraction conditions.

First, define P X j to be a function that takes a block j and projects it onto the orthogonal space of a previously retracted block i:

At t = 0, R i reduces to X i and Z j reduces to X j . Since X ⊤ i X j = 0, the second term of this expression vanishes and we are left with P X j (tV j ) satisfying the first retraction condition.

Finally, because we already showed that doing normalization and orthogonalization preserve first-order directions, it follows that applying these operations afterwards still preserves this direction and hence DR POB X (tV) ⃓ ⃓ ⃓ t=0 = V, which proves the validity of our retraction (Equation 14).

Notice that any block order also satisfies these retraction conditions, provided each block is projected onto the orthogonal complement of all previously processed blocks and then normalized if they are oblique or orthonormalized if it is the orthogonal block. Different orders may produce different finite updates but they always preserve the same first-order direction.

Generalization to an arbitrary number of oblique blocks

The previous construction of the orthoblique manifold can be extended to any number m of oblique blocks. Let p i be the number of factors in the ith oblique block and let r be the number of completely orthogonal factors, so that n = ∑︁ m i=1 p i + r. Then,

In this setup, the tangent space, normal space, and retraction would involve additional oblique blocks, pairs of oblique blocks, and pairs of oblique and orthogonal blocks. But all the formulas of these quantities follow by applying the same blockwise approach as before.

Furthermore, this generalization shows that orthogonal and oblique rotations are special cases of the orthoblique manifold. If there are no oblique blocks and r = n, then POB(∅, n) = O(n). And if there is one oblique block with p1 = n and r = 0, then POB({n}, 0) = OB(n).

Rotation constraints and standard errors

We already know how to impose constraints on X in orthogonal, oblique, and orthoblique rotation scenarios. A remaining matter is how these constraints are translated to the rotated loadings Λ and factor correlation matrix Ψ as this information is required for computing valid standard errors of rotated parameters.

Let θ be a vector collecting the k factor model parameter estimates after factor rotation and I θ be the corresponding observed information matrix (i.e., the negative Hessian of the log-likelihood). Define C θ as the k × c matrix whose columns contain the derivatives of the c constraints with respect to these parameters that must be satisfied at the stationary point.

Then, is an augmented Information matrix whose inverse can be used to obtain a variancecovariance estimate in maximum likelihood estimation (Jennrich, 1974, Equation 17).

For non-maximum-likelihood estimators, an alternative augmented Hessian matrix that replaces I θ with the Hessian matrix of the loss function can be incorporated into the sandwich covariance estimator described by Zhang (2014).

For orthogonal and oblique rotations, the derivatives of the constraints can be found elsewhere (Archer & Jennrich, 1973; Jennrich, 1973). For orthoblique rotation, we first derive the constraints on the rotated parameters and then compute their corresponding derivatives with respect to all the model parameters.

First, let ζ be the function that transforms the rotation matrix into the rotated parameters.

In orthoblique rotation,

By the definition of gradient, at any tangent direction V we should have where ∆f ΛΨ is the gradient of the complexity function with respect to the rotated parameters.

The second equality follows because at a given solution the complexity criterion must be stationary.

As shown before, the tangent space of the orthoblique manifold has the form V = X -⊤ Ω, where the blocks of oblique factors in Ω are arbitrary off-diagonal matrices, the block of orthogonal factors is skew-symmetric, and the intersections in Ω between different blocks of factors i and j satisfy Ω ij = -Ω ⊤ ji . Substituting this tangent parameterization into the differential of ζ gives

At the stationary solution, substituting these derivatives into Equation 17 then gives

Therefore, the orthoblique constraints imply that the matrix must be symmetric, with the submatrices corresponding to the blocks of oblique factors being also required to be diagonal.

Following the orthoblique definition in (Equation 12), if p is the number of oblique factors in the first block, q is the number of oblique factors in the second block, and r is the number of completely orthogonal factors in the third block, then the number of stationarity equations is given by the dimension of the tangent space,

The first two terms count the off-diagonal entries of each oblique block. The next three terms count the symmetry equations between different blocks, and the final term counts the symmetry equations within the orthogonal block. These are c stationarity equations that become zero at convergence and whose derivatives with respect to the rotated parameters are collected in the C θ term of the augmented Information representation (Equation 16).

Differentiating Equation 18yields

To obtain the appropriate k × c derivatives in C θ from this differential, let e i ∈ R k denote the ith standard basis vector, whose ith element is one and whose remaining elements are zero. For each model parameter i = 1, . . . , k in θ, we set the direction dθ = e i in turn and evaluate Equation 19along dθ to find the first p(p -1) + q(q -1) constraint derivatives corresponding to the off-diagonal entries of the oblique blocks. Finally, to find the remaining pq + pr + qr + r(r -1)/2 constraint derivatives of the symmetric entries corresponding to the intersections between blocks and the entries in the orthogonal block, simply evaluate dK -dK ⊤ in the same iterative fashion.

Software: orthoblique rotation with the latent package

A general rotation framework should allow researchers to compare and use different factor structures without developing a new optimization routine for each application. The GPArotation package already provides this framework for conventional orthogonal and oblique rotations, with a wide catalogue of rotation criteria. These are features retained in our latent package (Jiménez et al., 2026), with some additional capabilities. The relevant innovation here is the addition of the orthoblique manifold to restrict the factor-correlation structure as desired and the capability to apply different rotation criteria to different blocks of factors.

Rotation in the latent package is performed through the lrotate() function, which can receive an initial loading matrix and factor correlation matrix to be rotated. An example is presented below: # Orthogonal factor rotation with varimax: fit_rotation <-lrotate(lambda = unrotated_lambda, projection = "orth", rotation = "varimax")

Here, the choice of criterion is separate from the choice of geometry: rotation specifies the criterion, while projection = "orth" requests an orthogonal rotation. The default, projection = "oblq", allows all factors to be oblique.

Now suppose that the unrotated_lambda object has six columns. To choose orthoblique rotation we must use the oblique argument. For example, consider the following orthoblique Here, oblique = c(3, 3) automatically selects the orthoblique geometry and partitions the factors into two blocks of three factors each. As a result, the columns 1-3 of the factor solution form the first oblique block, and columns 4-6 form the second oblique block.

Correlations are allowed within each block, while correlations between blocks are effectively constrained to zero. With six factors, the column ordering and the correlation structure of the rotated solution become

The factor correlations are freely estimated within each oblique block, whereas correlations between blocks are constrained to zero. In this setup, there are many alternative choices for the oblique argument. This argument accepts any vector of positive integers specifying the sizes of consecutive oblique blocks, which may vary in number and size as long as their total does not exceed the number of columns (i.e., factors) in the unrotated loading matrix.

For example, with six factors, oblique = c(2, 2, 2) specifies three oblique blocks of two factors each, whereas oblique = c(3, 2) specifies two oblique blocks containing three and two factors, respectively. In the latter case, the remaining factor is assumed to be completely orthogonal. More generally, whenever the sum of the block sizes is smaller than the total number of factors, all remaining factors are constrained to be completely orthogonal (i.e., they belong to the orthogonal block).

As an alternative to lrotate(), the function lefa() for exploratory factor analysis takes the raw data or a covariance matrix as inputs and performs factor extraction first and rotation later. lefa() allows all the same arguments as lrotate() but contains additional choices for factor extraction with either maximum likelihood ("ml"), unweighted least-squares ("uls"), or diagonally-weighted least-squares ("dwls") options. For instance, to fit an oblique EFA model with six factors using maximum likelihood and oblimin, we can use the following command: require a mathematical argument or simulation design with results supporting this particular combination of criteria.

Another advantage of using lefa() over lrotate() is that it automatically computes the variance-covariance matrix and standard errors of the rotated parameters and uniquenesses using the derivatives of the corresponding rotation constraints for all available criteria, even mixed criteria.

Finally, the latent package can also estimate factor scores that preserve the orthoblique factor correlations. Currently, two existing methods implemented in the function latscores allow this: the constrained regression method of Green (1969) that finds the best correlationpreserving scores in the least-squares sense, and the method of ten Berge et al. (1999) that minimizes the determinant of the residual covariance matrix: # Compute the best factor scores in the least-squares sense:

Green_scores <-latscores(fit_efa, method = "Green") # Compute the factor scores that minimize the determinant of the residual # covariance matrix: tenBerge_scores <-latscores(fit_efa, method = "tenBerge")

The development version of latent is available at https://github.com/Marcosjnez/latent, including the source and documentation of the functions lrotate(), lefa(), and latscores(). The R package can be installed with the command pak::pak("marcosjnez/latent") that uses the pak library (Csárdi & Hester, 2026).

Alternative approaches towards an orthoblique structure

In this section, we compare orthoblique rotation with two alternative approaches and highlight their main differences.

First, one may argue that achieving an orthoblique correlation pattern is already possible in Exploratory Structural Equation Modeling (ESEM) (Asparouhov & Muthén, 2009). In ESEM, rotation is usually presented as an optimization problem with independent exploratory blocks of factors that can be either oblique or orthogonal. Take as an example the orthoblique pattern presented in Figure 1. In the ESEM framework, we can reproduce the same correlation pattern by building a block-diagonal matrix with three square blocks of independent rotation matrices of size 2 × 2 each:

If X 1 and X 2 are subjected to oblique rotation and X 3 to orthogonal rotation, then an orthoblique pattern is recovered for the factor correlation matrix:

However, in this scenario the number of estimated parameters is smaller than in the more general orthoblique rotation framework presented here. In the ESEM representation there is a total of five free parameters: two in each of the oblique blocks and one in the orthogonal block. In contrast, the orthoblique rotation matrix starts with 36 matrix elements and then imposes six unit-diagonal constraints and 13 zero-correlation constraints to yield 17 free parameters. Thus, in this example orthoblique rotation has 12 additional degrees of freedom in the rotation matrix. Moreover, orthoblique rotation finds a dense rotation matrix X that yields the desired orthoblique pattern whereas ESEM solves a more restricted optimization problem with structural zeros in the rotation matrix that corresponds to intersections of different blocks of factors. Consequently, even if the number of free parameters were the same in both approaches, orthoblique rotation searches over a larger set of rotation matrices and can attain a lower value of the rotation criterion than the more restrictive block-diagonal approach used in ESEM. In this respect, orthoblique rotation is a more exploratory approach than ESEM.

Second, we also highlight that using orthoblique rotation in tandem with target rotation is closely related to the extended target rotation proposed by Zhang et al. (2019). Their method incorporates expectations about both the loading matrix and the factor correlation matrix into the rotation criterion, so that selected trait-method correlations in a multitrait-multimethod (MTMM) analysis are rotated toward zero. More precisely, the extended target rotation minimizes the following criterion using oblique rotation: where ⊙ is the Hadamard product.

W Λ and T Λ denote the weight and target matrices for the loading matrix. In contrast, W Ψ and T Ψ denote the weight and target matrices for the factor correlations. T Ψ must be symmetric and W Ψ must be off-diagonal symmetric with nonnegative elements. Lastly, the scalar w represents the relative contribution of the second term (i.e., targets on factor correlations) with respect to the former (i.e., targets on factor loadings).

This rotation criterion can effectively penalize the unwanted factor correlations for a sufficiently large w and the result can be very similar to standard target rotation with the orthoblique manifold. However, in our orthoblique approach, the roles of the manifold and rotation criteria are separated: the target criterion is used only to orient the loading matrix, while the orthoblique manifold imposes the trait-method orthogonality exactly. Hence, orthoblique rotation can be used with any rotation criterion, not only target-based criteria.

Moreover, the correlations encouraged to be small in extended target rotation become exact structural zeros in orthoblique rotation. As such, an additional benefit of our approach is that the constant parameter w that weights the relative contribution of target loadings and target correlations is not required.

Empirical applications of the orthoblique rotation

We now illustrate the use of the orthoblique manifold with two empirical applications in which neither a fully orthogonal nor a fully oblique rotation would represent the intended factorcorrelation structure. The examples come from psychopathology and personality research, respectively.

In the psychopathology example, we use a MTMM design, where the same traits are measured using different informants. In these models, the associations between indicators may arise from the substantive traits but also from the method through which the measurements were obtained. Here, the orthoblique manifold can keep trait and method sources of common variance separated by imposing orthogonality between both blocks of factors while doing a completely exploratory estimation of the loadings.

In the personality example, the HEXACO hierarchy contains many items and factors. It is set.seed(2026) R <-mtmm$ABCD$R # Extract the correlation matrix efa <-lefa(sample.cov = R, sample.nobs = 2119, nfactors = 5, estimator = "uls", oblique = c(2, 3), rotation = "target", target = target)

The results showed that the estimated correlation between Externalizing and Internalizing was .251. The correlations were .298 between Children and Parents, .240 between Children and Teachers, and .302 between Parents and Teachers. In contrast, all six correlations between trait and method factors were exactly zero as expected by the orthoblique constraints. The full factor correlation matrix rounded to three decimals was with rows and columns ordered as Externalizing, Internalizing, Children, Parents, and Teachers, respectively.

The partially specified target rotation produced a sparse loading pattern (Figure 6). Among the 162 loadings targeted toward zero, 160 (98.8%) had absolute values below .20, with a median absolute value of .030. The method factors were particularly well defined: all 54 expected method loadings exceeded .20 in absolute value, with a median absolute loading of .464.

In contrast, the Externalizing indicators, apart from those assessing Attention Problems, loaded poorly on the merged trait factor. Of these 18 indicators, 16 had absolute loadings below .20, with a median of .079 and a range from .028 to .259. In contrast, 15 of the 18

Attention Problems indicators exceeded .20 on the same factor, with a median absolute loading of .433. Thus, the original Externalizing indicators contributed little to the merged trait factor, which was mainly defined by the indicators of Attention Problems.

Externalizing Internalizing

Children Parents Teachers 54 49 44 39 34 29 24 19 14 9 5 Moreover, all 18 original Externalizing indicators loaded more strongly on their corresponding method factor than on the merged trait factor, with a median absolute method loading of .530. Therefore, their weak trait loadings do not mean that they contributed little to the entire solution. Rather, their shared variation was represented more strongly by the method factors, while the merged trait factor mainly reflected Attention Problems and not Externalizing.

Overall, this exploratory solution revealed that conflating the indicators of Externalizing and Attention Problems in a single factor did not recover the intended broad representation of both sets of indicators. Thus, the Externalizing label overstated the breadth of this merged factor.

From a statistical perspective, the relevant feature of this example is that rotation criteria and restrictions on the factor correlation matrix are handled separately. Partially specified target rotation encourages an interpretable orientation for the loadings and permits empirically relevant cross-loadings, whereas the orthoblique manifold guarantees the exact block-orthogonality structure that disentangles the variance explained by traits and methods.

A conventional orthogonal rotation would have forced the correlations within the trait and method blocks to zero, while a conventional oblique rotation would have estimated the six trait-method correlations that must be excluded by the MTMM design. In both cases, the results would have been inconsistent with the theoretical model. Thus, the orthoblique manifold combined with partially specified target rotation made it possible to assess the adequacy of this MTMM analysis using EFA, revealing a potential model misspecification in Watts et al. (2022).

HEXACO. A hierarchical model with multiple oblique general factors

The second application uses the icases data obtained from the repository accompanying Anglim et al. (2022) at https://osf.io/72zp3/. We selected the MOOC sample (N = 4, 286), an international sample of participants who completed the HEXACO-100. The original study reports that the sample included participants from 131 countries, with 69% being female, and had a mean age of 34.7 years. For the present analysis, we used 96 HEXACO items corresponding to the six personality domains and their 24 facets, excluding the last four indicators of Altruism that do not belong to the HEXACO hierarchy (i.e., they are not expected to be nested in a general factor).

We fitted a 30-factor EFA model with six general factors, one for each HEXACO domain, and 24 group factors. The first six factors were specified as one oblique block and the 24 group factors were left orthogonal. The specified factor correlation matrix has the form where Ψ G contains the correlations among the six general factors.

Therefore, the general factors can correlate, whereas every group factor is orthogonal to the general factors and to all other group factors. In this way, common variance can be effectively partitioned between group factors, representing narrow facet-level variance, and general factors, representing broader variance shared across facets.

This formulation extends the exploratory bifactor approach of Jennrich and Bentler (2011; 2012) to the case of multiple correlated general factors. In their biquartimin and bigeomin criteria, a single general-factor column is excluded from the rotation criterion, which is applied to the group-factor columns only. Here, there are six general-factor columns, and their orientation also has to be determined. We therefore applied a separate criterion to the general factor columns and another to the group factor columns. Thus this example also illustrates the possibility of combining different rotation criteria within the same analysis.

To examine the extent to which the theoretical HEXACO structure is supported, we compared three rotation specifications. With 30 factors, a nontarget rotation has a large rotational search space and hence a greater risk of capitalizing on chance. Conversely, targeting a large number of loadings toward zero gives the theoretical structure a stronger role in determining factor orientation. In two analyses, we therefore applied geomin or oblimin to the six general factors and then partially specified targets on the 24 group factors. In these two target-based analyses, only the group factors were targeted, providing theoretical anchoring where capitalizing on chance was greatest while leaving the general factors exploratory. The third analysis applied oblimin separately to the block of general factors and to the block of group factors. This specification allows both levels to be oriented by the complexity criterion and can be viewed as an extension of biquartimin rotation to multiple general factors. 7The three solutions recovered different aspects of the theoretical HEXACO hierarchy. In both target-based solutions, all 96 items had their largest absolute loading among the 24 group factors on the expected facet. This occurred for 75 of 96 items (78.1%) with the nontarget oblimin rotation. Thus, target rotation provided clearer facet separation, although the nontarget oblimin solution also recovered much of the intended facet structure.

In all three solutions, the general-factor loading patterns broadly reflected the HEXACO domains. Emotionality, Extraversion, Conscientiousness, Openness, and Agreeableness were recognizable, whereas Honesty-Humility was less consistently separated in the targetbased specifications. Some Honesty-Humility items showed salient cross-loadings on the Conscientiousness general factor, and additional general-factor cross-loadings remained.

The following code illustrates the oblimin + target specification. Oblimin is applied to the six general factors and target rotation to the 24 group factors. The argument oblique = 6 automatically selects the orthoblique manifold and places the first six factors in one oblique block; all remaining factors are mutually orthogonal and orthogonal to the general factors.

The same oblique specification is used in all three analyses, independently of their rotation criteria. Because the items are ordinal, the model is estimated with diagonally weighted least squares using polychoric correlations (ordered = TRUE):

differences were modest, especially for the group-factor block.

The oblimin + oblimin solution (Figures 7d and 8c) recovered the expected group factor for 75 items. Its median absolute expected group-factor loading was .348, with 72 of 96 exceeding .20; 2,193 of the 2,208 group-factor cross-loadings were below .20, and five exceeded .30. In contrast, its general-factor block showed a clearer simple structure than in the two target-based solutions: the median absolute expected general-factor loading was .459, 95 of 96 exceeded .20, and 468 of the 480 general-factor cross-loadings were below .20. The absolute correlations among the general factors ranged from .015 to .276 (median .138), larger at the upper end than under the two target-based solutions. This configuration therefore favored a clearer general-factor structure at the expense of less distinct group factors.

In other words, the three solutions illustrate a trade-off between the general and group levels of the HEXACO hierarchy. Both target-based solutions yielded highly interpretable facet structures while leaving general-factor loadings exploratory. In contrast, the nontarget oblimin solution provided a particularly simple general-factor pattern but weaker facet recovery.

In any case, the conceptual advantage of the orthoblique manifold was particularly clear in this example. A fully oblique 30-factor rotation would allow all 435 factor correlations, although only the 15 correlations among the six general factors are needed for this representation.

Conversely, a fully orthogonal rotation would force these 15 correlations to zero. Taken together, these applications illustrate the distinction between rotation criteria and manifold restrictions. Complexity criteria can yield different sparsity and parsimony patterns for each block of factors whereas the orthoblique manifold defines the admissible factor-correlation geometry exactly. The two mechanisms can therefore be combined without turning the factor model into a confirmatory simple-structure model.

Discussion

Conventional factor rotation has treated factors as either completely orthogonal or completely oblique. We generalized this framework by introducing the orthoblique rotation, which allows factors to correlate within specified blocks while remaining orthogonal between blocks. This new rotation approach accommodates theoretically motivated or design-imposed constraints on the factor correlation matrix and hence expands the flexibility and modeling capabilities of EFA.

In this sense, orthoblique rotation helps bridge the gap between exploratory and confirmatory factor analysis by allowing researchers to impose selected factor-correlation constraints while retaining the exploratory estimation of all factor loadings. Thus, prior theoretical knowledge can be incorporated into the factor model without imposing a confirmatory loading structure, preserving the ability of EFA to uncover unexpected cross-loadings and departures from theoretical expectations.

To develop the orthoblique geometry, we provided a detailed and self-contained review of the GPA for orthogonal and oblique rotations, describing and connecting many mathematical concepts and visual representations that are often overlooked in discussions concerning factor rotation. In particular, starting from the defining constraints of the orthoblique manifold, we derived its tangent and normal spaces to find an expression for the Riemannian gradient and also designed a suitable retraction algorithm to calculate appropriate updates for the rotation matrix in the GPA. We hope that these explicit connections make factor rotation a less obscure matter in EFA and help researchers understand how further extensions can be constructed. We also showed that the conventional orthogonal and oblique rotation geometries emerge as special cases of the orthoblique framework, establishing orthoblique rotation as a natural generalization of both approaches.

The empirical applications illustrated two complementary uses of this new orthoblique geometry: an MTMM representation in which trait and method factors can correlate within their respective blocks while remaining orthogonal between blocks, and a bifactor model in which several general factors may correlate with one another while remaining orthogonal to group factors that are also mutually orthogonal. As such, the present work not only builds on the general-purpose rotation framework developed by Jennrich (2001), Jennrich (2002), and Bernaards and Jennrich (2005), but also extends the exploratory bifactor rotation methods based on biquartimin and bigeomin (Jennrich & Bentler, 2011 , 2012) to models with multiple general factors.

Additionally, we provided a new R package to exploit these new rotation capabilities.

The function lrotate() implements orthogonal, oblique, and orthoblique rotations and also allows combinations of different complexity criteria. Through the projection, oblique, and rotation arguments, researchers can specify a desired factor-correlation pattern with structural zeros and different complexity criteria for specific columns of the loading matrix. As a result, combining orthoblique rotation with multiple rotation criteria substantially expands the range of possible EFA specifications and opens up new opportunities for developing innovative approaches to exploring factor structures. The bifactor application with multiple both factor loadings and factor correlations.

Psychological Methods, 24 (3), 390-402. https://doi.org/10.1037/met0000198 # Oblique EFA with Maximum Likelihood and oblique oblimin rotation: fit_efa <-lefa(data = data.matrix, sample.cov = NULL, # Optional covariance matrix nfactors = 6L, estimator = "ml", projection = "oblq", rotation = "oblimin")

Because different factor blocks may contain different levels of complexity, both lrotate() and lefa() also allow the application of different rotation criteria to arbitrary columns of Λ. Suppose that we have a block of three oblique factors and another block of three orthogonal factors. Then, we may choose to apply oblimin to the oblique block to maximize item simplicity but varimax to the orthogonal block to maximize the variance of the squared loadings within factors. This rotation specification can be easily implemented by providing a list of rotation criteria to the rotation argument: # Orthoblique EFA with combined oblimin + varimax rotations: fit_efa <-lefa(data = data.matrix, nfactors = 6L, estimator = "ml", oblique = 3, rotation = list( oblimin = list(factors = 1:3), varimax = list(factors = 4:6)))

The value oblique = 3 automatically selects the orthoblique manifold and allows correlations only among the first three factors (i.e., oblique block). The remaining three factors are assumed to be completely orthogonal (i.e., orthogonal block). Independently, the rotation argument applies oblimin to the first three loading columns and varimax to the last three columns. Thus, oblique determines the permitted factor-correlation structure, whereas rotation specifies the complexity criterion applied to each loading block. As a clarification, this particular combination of oblimin for oblique factors and varimax for orthogonal factors is intended solely to illustrate the flexibility of the rotation framework in the lrotate() function and is not a recommendation for use in practice. The latter would therefore plausible that some items have secondary loadings or that some facets are less clearly defined than expected. EFA allows these possibilities to be examined instead of assuming that every item belongs exclusively to its designated domain and facet. However, the distinction between general and group factors still requires selected factor correlations to be zero. Thus, the exploratory model leaves all loadings free, whereas the orthoblique manifold constrains to zero the correlations between general and group factors, as well as all correlations between distinct group factors.

Thus, both examples can be studied exploratorily while preserving the theoretically specified zero correlations between theoretically unrelated factors.

We used partially specified target rotation for the MTMM application and fitted three models to the HEXACO data using different criteria for general and group factors, with combinations involving partially specified targets, geomin, and oblimin. The models with partially specified target rotation used the theoretical model to establish the targets.

All analyses were conducted with the latent package and the lefa() function. The R scripts used for the empirical applications, including all the materials used to generate the current manuscript, are available in an Open Science Framework (OSF) repository at https://osf.io/hszfc/overview?view_only=1bd218794e3d45e5ad27e551b5e4816d.

ASEBA. An exploratory multitrait-multimethod model

The first application uses the MTMM data analyzed by Watts et al. (2022). The correlation matrix was obtained from the supplementary materials available at https://osf.io/hvskz/. We used the ABCD sample (N = 2, 119), which includes child-, parent-, and teacher-reported psychopathology indicators measuring three traits: Attention Problems, Externalizing, and Internalizing. 6 The original authors included the Attention Problems indicators in an Externalizing factor (Watts et al., 2022). This decision had a substantive motivation. Although the Achenbach System of Empirically Based Assessment (ASEBA) does not include Attention Problems in its Externalizing composite, Watts et al. (2022) included these indicators because attentiondeficit/hyperactivity disorder is widely regarded as a marker of childhood Externalizing.

To justify this decision, they also referred to previous ASEBA analyses in which Attention Problems loaded strongly on an Externalizing factor.

An exploratory analysis can examine whether the Attention Problems indicators are adequately captured by the Externalizing factor, as assumed in the merged specification. We therefore considered five exploratory factors: two trait factors corresponding to Externalizing and Internalizing, where the Externalizing label now includes the items of the Attention Problems factor, and three method factors corresponding to children, parents, and teachers.

Each indicator is expected to reflect both a substantive trait and a measurement method, but this allocation need not hold exactly in the data. Some indicators may show secondary loadings or may represent their designated trait or method less clearly than expected. A partially specified target rotation allows these possibilities to be examined without fixing non-target loadings to zero. At the same time, the MTMM structure requires trait factors to remain uncorrelated with method factors, while correlations may be allowed among trait factors and among method factors. Thus, the target criterion provides the substantive orientation of the factors, whereas the orthoblique manifold constrains all trait-method factor correlations to zero.

The two trait factors and the three method factors form two internally oblique blocks such that where Ψ is the factor correlation matrix between the traits and Ψ M is the factor correlation matrix between the methods.

Thus, the Externalizing-Internalizing correlation and the three correlations among method factors are estimated, whereas all six trait-method correlations are constrained to zero by the orthoblique manifold.

to specify the model with the latent package, we must consider that factor positions 1 and 2 correspond to Externalizing and Internalizing, respectively, and positions 3, 4, and 5 correspond to Children, Parents, and Teachers. Supplying the argument oblique = c(2, 3) automatically selects the orthoblique manifold: the two trait factors may correlate with each other, the three method factors may correlate with one another, and all six trait-method correlations are then zero. The single criterion rotation = "target" is applied to the complete loading matrix, and target is the correspondingly partially specified target matrix for the 54 retained indicators. The model was fitted using unweighted least squares as follows: The oblimin + target solution yielded low absolute correlations among the six general factors ranging from .007 to .217, with a median of .107 (Figure 8b). All general-specific correlations and all off-diagonal correlations among the 24 group factors were zero, as expected by the orthoblique constraints. Therefore, of the 30(29)/2 = 435 possible factor correlations, only the 6(5)/2 = 15 correlations among the general factors were estimated, while the remaining 420 were fixed to zero.

The median absolute loading on the theoretically expected general factor was .384 and 85 of the 96 designated general-factor loadings exceeded .20 in absolute value whereas 430 of the 480 general-factor cross-loadings (89.6%) were below .20. For the 24 group factors, the median absolute theoretically expected loading was .431, and 91 of these 96 loadings exceeded .20. In contrast, 2,205 of the 2,208 group-factor cross-loadings (99.86%) were below .20, and no group-factor cross-loading exceeded .30.

With geomin + target (Figures 7b and 8a), the expected group-factor loadings had a median absolute value of .427, with 91 of 96 exceeding .20; 2,204 of the 2,208 cross-loadings on other group factors were below .20. Thus, geomin and oblimin produced comparably sparse group-factor patterns when paired with target rotation. For the general factors, the median absolute designated loading was .414, 82 of the 96 designated loadings exceeded .20, and 426 of the 480 general-factor cross-loadings were below .20. The absolute general-factor correlations ranged from .007 to .214 (median .116). Compared with oblimin + target, the general factors illustrated only a few of these combinations; we have not examined the many other possibilities or established which will be most useful in different substantive settings.

The latent package makes these unexplored combinations already available to researchers, who can investigate new rotation configurations based on their parameter recovery, stability, and interpretability.

In this sense, the current work provides both a geometric background and a software basis for further research on factor rotation and, more broadly, on EFA. However, an important limitation concerns the identification and ordering of factors in EFA. Factor solutions are identified only up to sign changes and permutations of the factors. Consequently, the factor positions specified as oblique blocks may not correspond to the substantive factors that researchers intended to allow to correlate. It is therefore important to inspect the rotated loading matrix to verify that the factors assigned to each oblique block correspond to the intended factor structure. Another limitation is that we did not assess the susceptibility of orthoblique rotation to local minima. In factor rotation, different starting values for the rotation matrix may lead to different stationary points, potentially yielding different loading and factor correlation patterns. Although the latent package supports conducting rotation from multiple random starting values, these do not guarantee convergence to the global minimum. Furthermore, the accuracy and stability of parameter recovery have not yet been systematically evaluated under different factor structures. Future simulation studies should therefore examine the robustness of orthoblique rotation to different starting values, rotation criteria, and population factor structures.
